> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5I2VPYPJJ68&lc=UgxY3X2mG8wgXM5hvrl4AaABAg.AKSkxTwq1AsAM196knr5nI)  
>
> *Intention:* “Guys, let this man breathe. He has as much right as anyone to look out for himself and his own.”  
>   
> *Potential unintended net effect on the collective consciousness:* “Guys, quell your drive for truth. Truth will reveal itself when it’s ready, so be quiet and obedient. Extreme politeness and passivity are more virtuous than demanding answers. Consider yourself lucky if truth ever finds you, but , rest assured, you’re powerless to discover it or help it spread without being blessed by that miracle.”  
>   
> I’m not saying it’s wrong to defend his right to choose what he contributes and at what pace (I agree wholeheartedly with your sentiment). I’m saying: don’t lose sight of what people like Prof. Jiang represents -- a “flame” that ignites the desire to uncover unfiltered truth and distill it into self-empowering knowledge, the kind that grounds real solutions to real problems, at both the individual and collective scales of humanity.   
>   
> We're talking about the kind of movement that can *literally* save humanity, if only enough people are involved!  
>   
> Bearing that in mind, we risk snuffing out that flame when we sound, if only perceptually, like we’re speaking against one's demand for truth, especially for people teetering on the edge of that path, still tethered to the comfort of commonly held lies that keep today’s society afloat. The path is costly already, without accounting for explicit dissent, since we're all aware that "rocking the boat" can end poorly for us; thus, the slightest nudge back toward comfort, which may well come in the form of a singular perceived dissenting voice, can be the "straw that [breaks] the camel’s back."  
>   
> This is why I argue that we should develop a sensitivity to go out of our way to not invalidate, or seem to look down on, the choice to seek truth -- particularly among those not yet fully committed to the path (which, let's face it, is most people who identify as "truth-seekers," let alone people who're playing around with the idea of peeking that direction). Furthermore, I'd argue that there are many contexts in which making mistakes and “behaving foolishly" are part of growth, and may even be the most efficient path to growth in some circumstances.   
>   
> It's from this perspective that I argue, if someone seems pushy or rude in how they implicitly or explicitly "demand answers" (when it comes to anything arguably in the category of "seeking-truth"), consider that they may be in a "phase" of truth-seeking where missteps are perhaps not only acceptable, but also necessary (for efficient growth). Framing it that way should help us resist the urge to correct their method and instead focus on being a positive force in  keeping their flame stoked.  
>   
> I believe I'm highlighting here an important bit of wisdom that says something like follows, "sometimes it's better to be silent than to try to teach 'right behavior,' because the very act of trying to make right sometimes has the unintended consequence of being a 'negative force of inertia' in one's natural (and/or most efficient) journey to growth." This may prove to be a misaligned approach in most contexts, but I'm convinced that it's very much aligned in this edge case, seeing as this is *such* a touchy, uncomfortable topic compared to most (for the majority of people out there) and people are, therefore, so much more likely to be swayed away from the path of growth (in this context) by any given encounter involving a "dissenting voice," even if the dissent perceived in said voice is solely the product of misperception.

> This perspective, laden with Christian Zionism and geopolitical maneuvering, misses the essential truth that history has already reached its apex with the finished work of Jesus Christ. The biblical narrative about the return of Israel and the future physical kingdom has been fulfilled in the spiritual reign of Christ, and those who continue to push for such earthly manifestations are not acknowledging that the kingdom of God is not of this world.
  
> 
  
> The full preterist perspective destroys this entire framework of Zionism as it is based on misunderstanding the nature of Christ’s victory. Jesus' work in establishing the Kingdom was fully accomplished in the first century, climaxing with the destruction of Jerusalem and the end of the old covenant in AD 70. All the types and shadows found in the Old Testament, including the promise of land and the physical return of the Jews to Israel, were fulfilled spiritually. The new covenant is not a geopolitical project but a spiritual reality in which all believers, Jew and Gentile alike, are brought into the Kingdom of God through Christ’s completed work.
  
> 
  
> The pursuit of physical fulfillment of ancient prophecies is a distraction from the true gospel. Christian Zionists today, like those in the past, are still laboring under the false notion that God’s promises to Israel depend on a return to a physical land, a flawed interpretation that ignores Christ’s actual, spiritual fulfillment of these promises. The real victory was not in a geopolitical Jerusalem but in the establishment of the New Jerusalem—the Church. The true people of God, now unified, are no longer confined to a physical location. The temple is not a building in Jerusalem; it is the body of Christ, His Church, where He resides through the Spirit.
  
> 
  
> As for the game theory perspective presented here, it’s based on worldly thinking, which is the very system Christ came to dismantle. The world is not governed by the rules of human power games but by the sovereign will of God. The so-called "game" that these players are engaged in is irrelevant because history is not a game; it’s a story written by God’s hand. The nations will come to Christ, and all rulers will be brought under His feet—not through military conquest, but through the preaching of the gospel. The true Christian Zionism is not about geopolitical maneuvering but about the spiritual realization of Christ's reign.
  
> 
  
> In rebuking this viewpoint, I urge those caught up in these ideas to reconsider the sufficiency of Christ’s work and to trust that His kingdom is already here, already victorious. The battle has been won. Those who continue to wait for a future event miss the point that Christ has already "come" in judgment, fulfilled the law, and established His kingdom—not in the Middle East, but in the hearts of believers worldwide. Let us stop trying to remake the world into a physical image of what it once was and instead bring people to the spiritual reality of Christ’s reign, which is both here and now and eternal.

I really enjoyed listening to this talk by Prof. Jiang. But as always, I listened with caution and didn’t just accept everything he said at face value. What was interesting and honestly, quite surprising was the realization that so much of what we think we know today is actually a narrative controlled by certain groups. While I’ve always known this, it still surprises me every time.



For example, I’ve always only known Isaac Newton as a brilliant scientist. But after hearing Prof. Jiang’s talk and doing a quick fact check using AI, I found that much of what he said about Newton’s theological beliefs and movements is actually verifiable. For instance, Newton based on his writings believed in a divine plan for the Jewish people, that the Temple in Jerusalem would one day be rebuilt, that the Jews would return to their homeland, and he even studied Hebrew and took a deep interest in Jewish history and rituals.

Challenge to the Professor:

You argue that there is little direct evidence from the Prophet Muhammad’s lifetime. Yet, a strong scholarly consensus—across both Eastern and Western traditions—holds that the Qur’an as we have it today is, with a high degree of certainty, the same text that was formulated and transmitted during the Prophet’s lifetime.

If that is the case, then your premise—that Muhammad may have presented himself as a Jewish-style messiah—appears fundamentally flawed. The Qur’an itself is unambiguous in distinguishing his role: it consistently refers to him as a messenger and prophet, while reserving the title of Messiah (al-Masīḥ) exclusively for Jesus.

Is it not contradictory to suggest that Muhammad claimed a messianic identity when the very text most reliably linked to him explicitly denies that characterization?

Some might argue he could’ve claimed messiahship early on—but even the earliest Quran verses contain no messianic language or reference to Jewish expectations like Davidic lineage. If we dismiss the Qur’an—the only early, well-preserved source—without any solid contemporary alternative, we’re in the realm of speculation, not history.

> We now have multiple early manuscripts that have been carbon dated to the 7th century, overlapping directly with the Prophet’s lifetime (570–632 CE). The Birmingham manuscript dates to 568–645 CE, and the Ṣan‘ā’ manuscript from Yemen to 578–669 CE — both containing verses that closely match the Qur’an we have today. These aren’t vague oral legends — they’re physical, scientific evidence.  
>   
> These carbon dated fragments are early, they match the modern Qur’an, and they show the text was already in circulation. There’s no trace of a radically different version.  
>   
> More importantly, the Qur’an wasn’t preserved by writing alone. It was memorized by hundreds, even thousands, during the Prophet’s life — a fact acknowledged even by many non-Muslim scholars. In a culture where oral transmission was central, and where Qur’anic recitation was public and constant, fabricating or altering the text later would’ve been impossible without immediate backlash.  
>   
> And finally, the full compilation was completed under Caliph Uthman around 650 CE — just two decades after the Prophet’s death. Even non-Muslim historians acknowledge that this dual method — oral and written — combined with the scale and transparency of transmission, is rare in history. And it’s why the full compilation under Caliph Uthman, just 20 years after the Prophet’s death, wasn’t a creation — it was a standardization of what was already widely known and preserved.  
>   
> All actual historical data — from manuscripts, oral tradition, and early compilation — points to one conclusion.   
>   
> It’s worth noting that there’s actually a lot more evidence from classical and contemporary Eastern and Muslim scholarship — including detailed manuscript studies, oral transmission chains, and historical records. But I’ve focused here on sources that are more familiar or accepted in the Western academic world, simply because some people still struggle to see rigorous, scientific scholarship coming from outside Western institutions. Really appreciate these videos as a Muslim! Just a couple of corrections and cool facts:

1. Muslims view Muhammad ﷺ as the final prophet, not the messiah. However, some Jews and Christians at the time did convert to Islam, believing he was the fulfillment of other prophetic signs or recognizing his truth through scripture or personal conviction. Jews were awaiting a messiah from the line of David who would establish a Jewish kingdom. Muhammad ﷺ did not fit that expectation. Christians already believed Jesus was the Messiah, so they did not view Muhammad ﷺ as fulfilling that role. 

2. In the Quran, In Surah Ar-Rum (The Romans) (30:2–4), God says:

“The Romans have been defeated. In a nearby land. But they, after their defeat, will overcome. Within a few years…”

At the time this was revealed, as the professor explained, the Byzantine Empire had been defeated by the Persians, but God reveals in the Qur’an their comeback, and it happened. Around 614, the Byzantines lost Jerusalem. By 627, under Heraclius, they defeated the Persians. We see this as one of God’s many fulfilled prophecies in Islam. 

3. Muslims believe Jesus is the Messiah (al-Masih in Arabic) and was born of the Virgin Mary. He is not divine in Islam, but a revered prophet. Muslims believe Jesus will return before the Day of Judgment, defeat the false messiah (Antichrist), and restore justice.

4. Muslims don’t believe as the professor said (though perhaps he didn’t mean it literally) that humans are children of God. Muslims believe all humans are servants of God by nature. Those who choose to willingly submit to God are called Muslim, which literally means “one who submits.” We also don’t believe “God is love” in the sense that it is His essence, we do believe that love is one of God’s attributes, and Islam teaches that God is loving.

My humble request to the professor is not to look at Islam from the lens of Christianity but instead on its own terms 🙏

### **@AbduSamad-v8c** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5I2VPYPJJ68&lc=Ugzu7FVqDKvQgjStLmd4AaABAg)

Here are some historical inaccuracies in the “Arabian Desert” section that you might want to address if you're aiming for an unbiased portrayal:



At 00:26:59 , you claim there was a process of syncretization where Jewish beliefs were being merged with Arab pagan beliefs.

This is misleading. The Jewish communities in Medina did not actively propagate their faith, but rather preserved it within their own communities. The connection between Arabs and Jews is primarily one of lineage - both trace their ancestry back to Abraham, with Arabs seen as descendants of Ishmael. Early Arab tribes were believed to be monotheistic, worshiping Allah as the one God, until idolatry was later introduced by Amr ibn Luhayy, chief of the tribe of Jurhum.

For example, Muslims interpret Deuteronomy 18:18–19 as referring to the Ishmaelites (i.e., Arabs), indicating a future prophet from among them - Muhammad ﷺ.


At 00:27:34 , you suggest there was a widespread belief in the Arabian desert that a messiah would come to unite the Jewish people.

This is inaccurate. At the time, most Arabs were pagans, with small Christian and Jewish minorities. There was no widespread messianic expectation among the general Arab population. The Jews, though present in certain regions like Medina, were a minority and maintained their distinct beliefs.

Around 00:30 , you state that Muhammad ﷺ "absolutely convinced that he is in fact the Jewish Messiah." There is no credible evidence supporting this claim. Muhammad ﷺ saw himself as a messenger sent to all of humanity, starting with the Arabs. 

Additionally, your statement that "a lot of his biography has been lost" is incorrect - he is among the most documented figures of his time. We have access to political treaties, letters to rulers such as Heraclius, Chosroes, and the Negus, and detailed accounts of his life and leadership.



Also, contrary to what you said, the majority of Jews in Medina did not accept Muhammad's prophethood and were eventually expelled or faced consequences due to breaches of treaty. 


Around 00:32:43, you make the false claim that Muhammad preached, “We are all the children of God.” This is a Christian doctrine and not what Muhammad taught. He believed that humans, like cats, dogs, and all of creation, are servants and creations of God, and that God “neither begets nor is born.”



At 00:33:41, you state, “Unfortunately, Muhammad died before he could complete his mission.” What exactly are you basing this on? Muhammad ﷺ clearly believed he had completed his mission before his death, a fact he emphasized in his Farewell Sermon.



At 00:34:02 , you claim that "Arabs were able to reconquer Jerusalem, return the Jews, and reconstitute the nation of Israel."

This is historically unfounded. While some Jewish families were allowed to return after the Islamic conquest of Jerusalem in 637 CE, they did not regain political authority or establish a nation-state. The conquest was for the spread of Islam, not to restore a Jewish homeland. Nevertheless, the Abrahamic linkage is there. 

At 00:35 , you say that "the companions of Muhammad were all basically eliminated in the power struggle after his death."

This is an exaggeration. While there were conflicts, such as the Battle of the Camel, many of the Prophet’s companions continued to lead the rapid expansion of the Islamic empire into Persia, Byzantium, and beyond. 


To uphold historical accuracy, how about you cross-check your claims with primary sources and engage with all relevant perspectives? This comprehensive approach would allow you to develop a more balanced and well-grounded analysis.

If someone curious about how Islam was described as promoting tolerance, while today it seems like the opposite, I would recommend the book by Fred Donner “Muhammad and the Believers” in which prof Donner argues that Muhammad early messages is ecumenical, accepting, to some extent, Jewish and Christian people to be part of Islamic community, on the condition that they affirm monotheism. Muhammad’s ideology/belief then should be viewed as “pan-monotheism”. 

Also there is one inscription that might support this theory: the “Yazid inscription”.

In this inscription, being etched on the stone after Muhammad’s death, during the time of the later Umayyad Caliphate, the author is presumably Christian soldier in the muslim-led caliphal army. The inscription, beginning with a sign of a cross, reads 

“May God be mindful of/remember Yazid, the king”.

This, together with the literary evidences, support that there are Christian, and to the extent Jews, in the army and government of the early Muslim empires.

### **@sonatraman**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5I2VPYPJJ68&lc=Ugw_ioe2dUypiLr5sPV4AaABAg)

Hello from the Middle East,
I discovered your channel through the Geo-Strategy series and really appreciate the way you explore history with depth and clarity.

In your recent video where you discussed the origins of Islam and the idea that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ became the Jewish Messiah, I found the perspective interesting — though it was unfamiliar to me. I’m planning to read the sources you recommended and to explore the topic further before forming a solid opinion. I also intend to watch your episode from the Civilization series called The Golden Age of Islam to better understand how you approach Islamic history overall.

That said, I did want to share a small reflection. As someone from this region, I naturally see Islam as emerging from the unique cultural, spiritual, and tribal context of the Arabian Peninsula. So hearing it presented primarily through a different historical lens felt a bit unusual — but that’s also why I want to learn more, not dismiss it.

Also, I just wanted to mention that although you said the Prophet’s biography was “lost,” many of his companions made great efforts to preserve his words and actions (the Hadiths), which are a major source of Islamic tradition and history.

I’m just out of high school, and I’m still learning. If I’ve misunderstood anything, I’d truly appreciate any correction. Thank you for the thought-provoking content — it’s opened up new paths of inquiry for me.

### **@SaddamHussain-hq6zf**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5I2VPYPJJ68&lc=UgzW6lx8YKntoZQy8Oh4AaABAg)

The professor made a very insightful point at minute 27:00 about how the Jews went into the desert, and how their beliefs later inspired the Arabs—who also believed that a Messiah would come and help them reclaim Jerusalem. Notably, this was during a time when only two major powers existed in the world: the Byzantine Empire and the Persian Sassanid Empire.

In the early 7th century, a man named Muhammad claimed to be a prophet of God. He faced tremendous opposition from his own people and was eventually forced to leave the city of Mecca and seek refuge in Medina to save his life. This event, known as the Hijrah, took place in 622 AD.

Prophet Muhammad claimed that if people—including Jews, pagans, and Christians—followed his teachings, they would gain success and even dominate the world. Otherwise, they would eventually be defeated.

We know that Prophet Muhammad’s rule was firmly established in Arabia, and after his passing, his followers not only continued his mission but also expanded their influence. Eventually, the Muslim empire overcame both the Byzantine and Sassanid empires, becoming the most powerful empire.  in the world.

Ironically, had the Jews accepted Prophet Muhammad, they too might have shared in that rise to global power—fulfilling their long-held belief that a messiah would come and that they would rule the world. 
Jerusalem might have been granted to them without any Roman threat. In 637 AD, Muslims took control of Jerusalem under the leadership of Umar ibn al-Khattab, a close companion of the Prophet.

Importantly, Umar allowed the Jews to return to Jerusalem and permitted them to worship at the Temple Mount and the Western Wall.

For many people, the only reason the bible is most memorized is because it’s a story that is imposed on us by authority figures, repeated on a weekly basis in the churches we attended (sometimes involuntarily because it was a family ritual) and was also told to us in a very dramatic, memorable way by a man in ceremonial robes standing before us in an impressive pulpit.  The ritual was symbolic and a community experience.  However, when I watched Star Wars at the movies and read Lord of the Rings, those stories took precedence in my own imagination, far beyond all the programming I’d received as a child.  Encountering those stories helped me break from my identity as strictly a Christian and into an identity that was more complex and multi-layered.

Thank you for your explanation in the video. However, I found myself a bit confused by your statements regarding Prophet Muhammad (PBUH).

According to the Qur’an, which was revealed through the Prophet Muhammad (PBUH), Chapter 112 (Surah Al-Ikhlas) is very clear in its message. The chapter, consisting of just four verses, explicitly states that God “begets not, nor is He begotten,” indicating that He has no children. This seems to directly contradict the explanation provided in your video.

Additionally, it's well-documented that the majority of the Jewish community at the time rejected Prophet Muhammad (PBUH) as a prophet. Given this historical context, it's difficult to understand how they would have accepted him as the Messiah. Would you be able to provide specific evidence to support that claim?

Thank you for your time and for helping clarify these points. 


Here is some more evidence showing that the jews did not except him as the messiah. 
The Constitution of Medina (622 CE):
The Prophet (PBUH) formed an agreement with the Jewish tribes in Medina (Banu Qaynuqa, Banu Nadir, and Banu Qurayza). While they agreed to political cooperation, they did not accept him religiously as a prophet or messiah.

Sīrah Literature (Ibn Ishaq’s Sirat Rasul Allah):
Ibn Ishaq recounts detailed instances of debates between the Prophet and Jewish rabbis who rejected his prophethood despite acknowledging that he fit the descriptions in their scripture.

3. Jewish Expectations of the Messiah
Traditional Jewish belief holds that the Messiah must be a Jewish king descended from David, who restores the Temple in Jerusalem and brings political sovereignty back to Israel.
Since Prophet Muhammad (PBUH) did not match these expectations (and was an Arab, not from the line of David), Jewish scholars and tribes at the time could not reconcile him with their concept of the Messiah

### **@AlixPrappas**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5I2VPYPJJ68&lc=UgxTca5R7wKWJKSHUjp4AaABAg)

Purely speaking logically (as in, using the laws of logic; law of IDENTITY, NON-CONTRADICTION, and EXCLUDED MIDDLE), the statement "all these religious differences are idiotic" is actually idiotic (showing poor judgement or intellectual warrant).

The Creator is the ground of being, Truth Himself.  Falsity is the enemy of truth by definition.  Therefore, the Creator, in a desire to share the goodness of His being, desires to teach what is true and dispel falsity.  Furthermore, there is ONE truth, ONE reality.  Therefore, the complete and accurate account of reality is ONE.  There can be partial accountings to various degrees in the same way that there can be various theories to attempt to describe a physical phenomenon with varying levels of explanatory power and scope.  The true theory, however and once more stated, is ONE.

Next, the religious differences you reference rest on fundamentally different epistemological and metaphysical frameworks, MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE frameworks as well.  Jews say Christ is not the messiah.  Christians say He is.  Both cannot be true.  Muslims say that Jesus is not God, Christians say He is.  Both cannot be true.  Many Hindu denominations say that all are different aspects of God Himself.  the Abraham-derived faiths say that nothing in creation shares in God's ontological divinity.  Both cannot be true.

So, with that framework in mind, let us return to the beginning of the comment.  If the Creator desires to teach what is true, and there is one truth, then He desire one truth taught.  By definition, then, all worldviews that conflict with the truth, He wants to be corrected of their errors and brought into alignment with the truth.

This describes the history of civilization.  The syncretistic notion of "all religions are equally valid roads to God" is non-sensical.  For, said in another way: 

VARIOUS CONTRADICTORY WORLDVIEWS WITH MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE TRUTH CLAIMS CANNOT LEAD TO THE SAME SINGULAR TRUTH BY DEFINITION.  

Furthermore, to "reach" God or to know Him means to know Him as He is.  If someone believes false things about Him, then they, by definition, do not know Him in His fullness.

As an analogy, I can know you very well.  However, if I believe something erroneous about you, then I don't know you as fully as I can.   God desires for us to know Him fully.  This means discarding false ideas and practices founded upon those ideas.  Moreover, expounding upon the analogy of "know(ing) you very well", if I truly love you, then I want others to know you for who you are and I have a duty to dispel rumors, calumny, or false accusations levied against you.  If this is good, pro-social behavior towards you, a human, how much more so in reference to God?

Therefore, if we love God and Jesus is God, shouldn't we want to convince others to give Him due glory? ### **@jogurt4625**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5I2VPYPJJ68&lc=Ugyo62xxoUis9rErc_d4AaABAg)

Muhammad was not and is not the Jewish Messiah. From the perspective of Judaism, Muhammad did not meet the messianic criteria required by the Torah (the Old Testament). According to Jewish tradition, the Messiah must be a Jew and a descendant of King David who will bring world peace, rebuild the Third Temple in Jerusalem, and gather all Jews back to Israel. Muhammad was an Arab, not a Jew.

The Jewish community believed that the era of classical biblical prophets had long since ended and that no further prophets (especially from another nation) would appear. Jews did not recognize Muhammad as a religious prophet, even under the Constitution of Medina. The Jewish tribes in Medina accepted the document as a political-military treaty; they recognized Muhammad as a political leader, judge, and arbiter tasked with establishing order and settling disputes in the oasis.

While the text of the treaty referred to Muhammad as the 'Prophet' and 'Messenger of God' (as he dictated the document), for the Jewish signatories, this was a title reflecting his status within his own Muslim community, not a commitment to their own belief in his prophecy. Muhammad’s teachings differed in certain details from the Jewish Torah, which Jewish scholars in Medina viewed as evidence that his message did not originate from the same divine source.

The fact that the Jews of Medina refused to recognize Muhammad as a prophet and often publicly questioned his teachings led to a gradual increase in tension. This friction eventually escalated into open hostility, the breach of the treaty, and the subsequent expulsion or elimination of the Jewish tribes from Medina."

Hello Prof. Jiang, good analysis but there are some considerable inaccuracies in the story of Muhammad:

1- historically the main reason Jews did not follow him was that the Jews wanted their promised prophet to come from within them so they would have absolute power as a community and rule with it. 

You see this in the narrated conversation between Huyayy Ibn Akhtab, master of the Jews of Bani Nadhir (a large faction that was influential and wealthy and controlled a lot of the economy of Medina and surrounding areas), and his brother “Abu Yasser”. Huyayy’s daughter Safiyya, then a child, was walking behind them and the men were walking slowly and full of sorrow. 

-Huyayy’s brother told Huyayy: “is he the one [prophet we are waiting for, whom we got news and of coming in our scriptures]?” 
-to which Huyayy replied “yes, by God [he is him]”.
-Abu Yasser said “did you recognize it from his actions and the signs we were told to look for?” 
-Huyayy replied “yes”.
-Abu Yasser asked “so what do you feel inside you toward him?”
-Huyayy replied “enemity, by God, for as long as I stay in this life”

Many years later, Muhammad actually married Safiyya.

2- Muhammad’s primary goal was not of pure materialism and pragmatism. When he started relaying the message to the people of Mecca the heads of the tribes offered to make him their ruler and pledged wealth and women and control to him, which he refused. He was a relayer of a message, a message that admonished and corrected even the prophet himself when he was mistaken or could have acted better. While he sought reconciliation with Jews and Christians, he corrected them in things they were “experts” in with evidence they could not refute, such as rejecting the lie that God has children, about which he relayed many messages from God via the Quran. Some examples: 

112:1–4
{Say, [O Prophet], “He is Allah, the Unique One. Allah, the sustainer [needed by all]. He has never had offspring, nor was He born. And there is none comparable to Him.}

4:48
{Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating others with Him [in worship], but forgives anything else of whoever He wills. And whoever associates others with Allah has indeed committed a grave sin.}

19:88–95 (the chapter named after Virgin Mary)
{They say “the Most Compassionate has offspring”. You have certainly made an outrageous claim— by which the heavens are about to crack/burst open, the earth to split apart, and the mountains to drop/crumble down to pieces, in protest of attributing children to the Most Compassionate. It does not befit [the Majesty of] the Most Compassionate to have children. There is none in the heavens or the earth who will not return to the Most Compassionate in full submission. Indeed, He fully knows them and has counted them meticulously. And each of them will return to him on the Day of Resurrection all alone.}

3- Islam acknowledges that Muslims, Christians and Jews are children of Abraham, but Islam strongly rejects the notion that we are children of God. In the Quran (5:16), God said 
{The Jews and the Christians say “we are the children of Allah and his most beloved!” Tell them: “Then why does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making. He forgives whoever He wells and punishes whoever He wills. And to Allah [alone] belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and what is between them. And to him is the final return.”}

4- You mention that not much was known about him, which is not true. He has one of the most (if not the most) documented lives of any human— we have narrations from his companions through so many paths of almost everything he said. To the point where the pauses of his speech, his facial expressions, his complexion when his emotion changed, his tone, his detailed appearance, even the number of white hairs on his chest and beard when he grew older, the things he nodded in approval of, the things he spoke against, the things he didn’t speak against (hinting that he approved of them), etc. 

Hundreds of scholars after his death spent their lifetimes collecting and tracing and verifying the narrations. They had a library of everyone the prophet interacted with, who he met and who he didn’t meet, who of his companions met who where and by who’s account, who travelled where, etc, to study the plausibility of each of the claimed narration paths. The number of independent converging narration paths through a chain of people known to be trustworthy (having not manipulated or mis-conveyed information in their record) determined the strength of the narration to the original source (the prophet). There are over 600,000 narrations from different paths total as of ~100 years after the prophet’s death.

I have been studying his history for years from original sources and you are the first person I heard to claim that “nobody knows much about him” which is misguided against the facts and I am curious as to how you reached this conclusion.
